      Representations, Sanctions, Counter Terrorism and Anti-terrorism
The Contractor must comply with its obligations (if any) under Part 4 of the Charter of the United Nations Act 1945 (Cth) and the Charter of the United Nations (Dealing with Assets) Regulations 2008 (Cth).
The Contractor must comply with all applicable Legislative Requirements dealing with the supply and/or export of goods, services and information to foreign nationals or institutions, including under the Customs Act 1901 (Cth) and the Weapons of Mass Destruction (Prevention of Proliferation) Act 1995 (Cth).
The Contractor acknowledges that it is an offence to knowingly make any funds or assets available to a person or organisation on the list of persons and entities designated as terrorist, available at https://www.dfat.gov.au/international-relations/security/sanctions/Pages/consolidated-list ('Consolidated List'). The Contractor must not deal with any such person or organisation.
The Contractor must, and must ensure that its agents, subcontractors, and employees:
ensure that individuals or organisations involved in implementing this Contract are in no way linked, directly or indirectly, to organisations and individuals associated with terrorism, including ‘terrorist organisations' as defined in Division 102 of the Criminal Code Act 1995 (Cth) and listed in regulations made under that Act and regulations made under the Charter of the United Nations Act 1945 (Cth); and
ensure that payments provided by the Principal are not used in any way to provide direct or indirect support or resources to organisations and individuals associated with terrorism.
in carrying out its obligations under this Contract, comply with laws in relation to sanctions including the Charter of the United Nations Act 1945 (Cth) and regulations made under that Act; the Autonomous Sanctions Act 2011 (Cth) and regulations made under that Act; and
ensure that payments provided by the Principal are not used in any way to provide direct or indirect support or resources to organisation and individuals for whom Australia has imposed sanctions under the Charter of the United Nations Act 1945 (Cth) and regulations made under that Act or the Autonomous Sanctions Act 2011 (Cth) and regulations made under that Act; and 
inform the Principal, if during the term of this Contract, it discovers any link whatsoever with any organisation or individual listed in or the World Bank List or a Relevant List including DFAT's Consolidated List.
If the Contractor, its personnel or subcontractors become aware or should have reasonably become aware that funds or resources have directly or indirectly been provided to organisations or individuals associated with terrorism, the Contractor must inform the Principal immediately. Any failure by the Contractor to provide such notice shall be a "substantial breach" for the purposes of subclause 39.2 of the Contract
      Prohibited Dealings
The Contractor must ensure that funds provided under this Contract (whether through a subcontract or not) are not used in any way to provide or facilitate direct or indirect support, resources or assets to individuals, entities or organisations:
listed on the World Bank List or any similar List;
listed on the Consolidated List;
listed on the Australian Government list of terrorist organisations at https://www.nationalsecurity.gov.au/what-australia-is-doing/terrorist-organisations/listed-terrorist-organisations; or
directly or indirectly engaged in, preparing, planning, assisting in or fostering the doing of a terrorist act.
The Contractor warrant that neither the Contractor nor its Key Personnel will make or cause to be made, receive or seek to receive any offer, gift or payment, or benefit of any kind, which could be construed as an illegal or corrupt act, either directly or indirectly to any Party, in relation to this Contract.
A breach of this clause 57 will be a "substantial breach" for the purposes of subclause 39.2 of the Contract.
      Fraud
The Contractor must not, and must ensure that that its agents, subcontractors, and employees, including key personnel do not, engage in any fraud and must prevent and detect fraud, including fraud by its personnel.
Within one month of the Date of the Contract, the Contractor must conduct a fraud risk assessment and produce a fraud control strategy in compliance with the Commonwealth Fraud Control Framework available at https://www.apsc.gov.au/initiatives-and-programs/workforce-information/research-analysis-and-publications/state-service/state-service-report-2023-24/integrity/commonwealth-fraud-and-corruption-control-framework. The risk assessment and strategy must contain appropriate fraud prevention, detection, investigation and reporting processes and procedures. 
The Contractor is responsible for preventing and detecting fraud including fraud within those functions performed by a subcontractor relating to the performance of the Works under this Contract. The Contractor must ensure that its agents, subcontractors, and employees, including Key Personnel, are responsible and accountable to the Contractor for preventing and reporting any fraud as part of their routine responsibilities. The Contractor must ensure that Personnel who are not employed by the Contractor report fraud to the Contractor within 7 days.
If the Contractor, its agents, subcontractors, and employees, including Key Personnel becomes aware of a fraud, it must report the matter to the Principal in writing within 7 days. The written report to the Principal must be signed by a Contractor authorised person and must include the following (where known):
the name of the Contract under which the Principal’s payment(s) is being provided;
name of any personnel (including any subcontractors) involved;
the allegation(s), including a chronological account of the facts giving rise to the allegation(s);
the names of the suspected offender(s) (where known);
details of witnesses;
copies of relevant documents;
references to any relevant legislation;
a nominated contact officer; 
any other relevant information (for example, political sensitivities, any other party or agency that has been informed, involved or that can assist with investigations); and
the current status of any inquiries commenced by the Contractor.
If a report has been made in accordance with this clause 58, the Contractor must respond within seven days to any further requests for information the Principal may make.
If the Contractor, its agents, subcontractors, and employees, including Key Personnel becomes aware of a fraud, the Contractor must, in consultation with Principal, develop and implement a strategy to investigate the fraud based on the principles set out in the Australian Government Investigations Standards.
The Contractor must investigate the fraud at the Contractor’s cost and in accordance with any directions or standards required by Principal. Whether the Contractor conducts an investigation or appoints an investigator to conduct an investigation, the person conducting the investigation must possess the minimum qualifications specified in the Australian Government Investigation Standards or an equivalent agreed to by the Principal.
The Principal reserves the right to appoint its own investigator, conduct its own investigation or report fraud to the appropriate law enforcement agencies or any other person or entity the Principal deems appropriate for investigation. If the Principal exercises these rights, the Contractor must provide all reasonable assistance that may be required at its sole expense.
After the investigation is finished, if a suspected offender has been identified or at the direction of the Director of the Principal’s Customer Fraud Control Section, the Contractor must promptly report the fraud to the local police and any other appropriate law enforcement agency where the incident occurred, unless the Director of Customer’s Fraud Section agrees otherwise in writing.
If the investigation finds the Contractor or employees including Key Personnel have engaged in fraud, the Contractor, in consultation with the Principal, must take all action necessary to recover, replace or return to the Principal any misappropriated funds or property (including paying to the Principal the full value of misappropriated funds and returning or replacing misappropriated or damaged property).
If the investigation finds that a person other than the Contractor or employee including Key Personnel has engaged in fraud, the Contractor must, at the Contractor’s cost, take all reasonable action to recover any Principal funds or Principal-funded property acquired or distributed through the fraud, including taking recovery action in accordance with recovery procedures (including civil litigation) available in the relevant country.
The Contractor must keep the Principal informed, in writing, on a monthly basis, of the progress of its efforts to recover the Principal's payments or Principal-funded property, including any recovery action.
If the Contractor considers that all reasonable action has been taken to recover the Principal’s payments or the Principal's-funded property and recovery has not been achieved or recovery has only been achieved in part, the Contractor may seek approval from the Principal that, no further recovery action be taken.
If an investigation finds that the Contractor or employees including Key Personnel have engaged in fraud, or if the Principal discovers that a fraud has not been reported to the Principal, the Principal reserves the right to:
terminate this Contract by giving the Contractor notice in writing specifying the date on which the termination will take effect, in which case the consequences of the termination will be as set out in subclause 39.10; and
not enter into any further agreements with the Contractor until such time as the Principal is satisfied that any recommended changes to the Contractor’s management and procedures have been made in order to prevent any further fraud from occurring and to ensure timely reporting of fraud to the Principal.
Without limitation to any of the Principal’s rights pursuant to subclause 39.10 (Termination), if the Contractor fails to comply with its obligations under this clause 58 with respect to a fraud, the Principal may give the Contractor a written notice which specifies:
the obligation/s with which the Contractor has not complied;
the action that the Contractor must take to rectify the failure; and
the date by which the contractor must rectify the failure.
If the Contractor does not comply with a notice issued, the Principal reserves the right to:
terminate this Contract in accordance with subclause 39.10 (Termination); and/or
withhold payments (or parts of payments) due to the Contractor, until the Contractor has complied with the notice.
This clause 58 survives the termination or expiration of this Contract, including with respect to any Fraud relating to the performance of this Contract, which is not detected until after this Contract has been terminated or has expired.
      Anti-Corruption
The Contractor warrants that, to the best of the Contractor’s knowledge as at the Date of the Contract, no money or other consideration of any kind paid or payable under this Contract or by separate agreement is, has been or will be used for unlawful purposes, including purposes violating anti-corruption legislation, including making or causing to be made payments to any employee of either Party or anyone acting on their behalf to assist in obtaining or retaining business to any person, or securing any improper advantage.
The Contractor warrants that neither it nor its agents, subcontractors, and employees, including Key Personnel will make or cause to be made, receive or seek to receive any offer, gift or payment, or benefit of any kind, which could be construed as an illegal or corrupt act, either directly or indirectly to any party, in relation to the execution of this Contract.
Any breach of this clause 59 (Anti-Corruption) will entitle the Principal to issue a notice under subclause 39.10 (Termination) to terminate this Contract immediately.
     Child Protection
The Contractor (and any Subcontractor) must comply with all relevant Australian and local legislation and labour laws in relation to child labour.
The Contractor must also comply with, and ensure that its officers, employees, subcontractors and agents comply with, DFAT's ‘Child Protection Policy', accessible on the DFAT website at: www.dfat.qov.au.
If any part of the activities carried out by the Contractor under the Contract involves the Contractor employing or engaging a person (whether as an officer, employee, agent, subcontractor, or volunteer) that is required by any law in the State or Territory in which the Works is to be conducted to have a working with children check to undertake such activities or any part of such activities, the Contractor agrees:
without limiting its other obligations under the Contract, to comply with all State, Territory or Commonwealth law relating to the employment or engagement of people who work or volunteer with children in relation to such activities, including mandatory reporting and working with children checks however described; and
if requested, provide the Principal at the Contractor's cost, a statement of compliance with this clause, in such form as may be specified by the Principal.
When child safety obligations may be relevant to a subcontract made in connection with the Contract, the Contractor must ensure that any such subcontract entered into by the Contractor for the purposes of fulfilling the Contractor's obligations under the Contract imposes on the subcontractor the same obligations regarding child protection that the Contractor has under the Contract. Each subcontract must also require the same obligations (where relevant) to be included by the subcontractor in any secondary subcontracts.
     Preventing sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (PSEAH)
When undertaking the Works, the Contractor must comply, and must ensure that its contractors, subcontractors, agents and personnel comply, with DFAT’s Preventing Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (PSEAH) Policy, including the reporting obligations, accessible at http://www.dfat.gov.au/pseah.
The Principal may conduct a review of the Contractor's compliance with DFAT’s PSEAH Policy. The Principal will give reasonable notice (at least 14 calendar days) to the Contractor and the Contractor must participate co-operatively in any such reviews.
If the Principal finds that the Contractor has failed to comply with DFAT’s PSEAH Policy, the Contractor must promptly, and at the cost of the Contractor, take such actions as are required to ensure compliance standards in the PSEAH Policy.
In reporting to the Principal, as required under this clause 61, the Contractor must comply with the Privacy Act 1988 (Cth) and the privacy provisions in the DFAT SEAH Incident Notification Form, available on DFAT’s website.
      Modern Slavery and Human Trafficking
The Contractor must take reasonable steps to identify, assess and address risks of Modern Slavery practices in the operations and supply chains used in the provision of the Works.
If at any time the Contractor becomes aware of Modern Slavery practices in the operations and supply chains used in the performance of the Contract, the Contractor must as soon as reasonably practicable take all reasonable action to address or remove these practices, including where relevant by addressing any practices of other entities in its supply chains. Incidents and the process taken to identify and address risk (form of a Modern Slavery risk management plan) must be reported to the Principal as soon as practical by contacting the Superintendent.
For the purposes of this clause 61, 'Modern Slavery' has the meaning given in the Modern Slavery Act 2018 (Cth).
      Environmental Requirements
The Contractor must ensure that in carrying out the Works:
it complies with all Legislative Requirements, the Principal's requirements and other requirements of the Contract for the protection of the environment;
it complies with all directions of the Principal or the Superintendent in relation to the environment; and
it immediately notifies the Superintendent of:
any non-compliance with the requirements of this clause 63;
any breach of any law for the protection of the environment; or
the receipt of any notice, order or communication received from any relevant authority for the protection of the environment.
      Pandemic Relief Event 
If the Contractor considers that there has been a Pandemic Relief Event, it must promptly notify the Superintendent in writing, providing details of the Pandemic Relief Event, and follow the instructions given by the Superintendent.
Where the Superintendent has determined a Pandemic Relief Event has occurred, the Contractor and the Superintendent are to agree the costs incurred by the Contractor because of the Pandemic Relief Event, excluding delay costs (if any) which are to be compensated under subclause 34.9 of the Contract, and if unable to agree, the Superintendent is to determine the costs.
To the extent permitted by Legislative Requirements, the Contractor will not be entitled to make (nor will the Principal be liable upon) any Claim arising out of or in connection with a Pandemic Relief Event or any instruction under paragraph (a), other than under this clause 64.
The Superintendent will reduce any entitlement the Contractor would have otherwise had under paragraph (b) to the extent that the Contractor has failed to take all reasonable steps to mitigate the costs referred to in paragraph (b).
For the purposes of this clause 64:
'Pandemic' means the disease known as Coronavirus (COVID-19) which was characterised to be a pandemic by the World Health Organisation on 11 March 2020; and
'Pandemic Relief Event' means a disruption which has an adverse effect on the supply of labour, equipment, materials or services required for the carrying out of the WUC caused as a direct result of the Pandemic, provided that the adverse effect is one which the Contractor could not have avoided or overcome by the taking of all reasonable steps (but without the need to expend additional costs).
   Commonwealth Supplier Code of Conduct
The Contractor must comply with, and ensure that its officers, employees, agents and Subcontractors comply with, the Code in connection with the performance of the Contract. 
The Contractor must:
periodically monitor and assess its, and its officers’, employees’, and agents’ compliance with the Code; and
on request from the Principal, promptly provide information regarding:
the policies, frameworks, or systems it has established to monitor and assess compliance with the Code; and
the Contractor’s compliance with paragraph (a) above.
The Contractor must immediately notify the Principal in writing upon becoming aware of any breach of paragraph (a) above. The notice must include a summary of the breach, the date that the breach occurred and details of the personnel involved.
Where the Principal identifies a possible breach of paragraph (a) above, it may notify the Contractor in writing, and the Contractor must, within 3 days of receiving the notice, either:
where the Contractor considers a breach has not occurred – advise the Principal that there has not been a breach and provide information supporting that determination; or
where the Contractor considers that a breach has occurred – notify the Principal under paragraph (b) above and otherwise comply with its obligations under this clause 65.
A failure by the Contractor to comply with its obligations under any part of this clause 65 will be a breach of the Contract.
Nothing in this clause or the Code limits, reduces or derogates from the Contractor’s other obligations under the Contract. The Principal’s rights under this clause are in addition to and do not otherwise limit any other rights the Principal may have under the Contract. The performance by the Contractor of its obligations under this clause will be at no additional cost to the Principal.
The Contractor acknowledges that the Principal may take the Contractor’s compliance with the Code into account in any registration of interest process, tender process or similar procurement process in connection with any other Commonwealth project.
For the purposes of this clause, 'Code' means the Commonwealth Supplier Code of Conduct dated 1 July 2024 accessible from the following link: https://www.finance.gov.au/government/procurement/commonwealth-supplier-code-conduct/commonwealth-supplier-code-conduct (as amended from time to time).
   Notification of Significant Events
The Contractor must immediately issue the Principal a notice on becoming aware of a Significant Event, summarising the Significant Event (including the date on which it occurred and specifying whether the personnel involved were involved in the Works). 
The Principal may notify the Contractor in writing that an event is to be considered a Significant Event for the purposes of this clause, and where this occurs the Contractor must issue a notice under paragraph (a) above in relation to the event within 5 days of being notified by the Principal.
If requested by the Principal, the Contractor must provide the Principal with any additional information regarding the Significant Event within 5 days of the request or prepare a draft remediation plan and submit that draft plan to the Principal for approval within 14 days of the request.
A draft remediation plan prepared by the Contractor under paragraph (c) above must include any such details as reasonably requested by the Principal. 
The Principal will review the draft remediation plan and either approve the draft remediation plan or provide the Contractor with the details of any changes that are required. The Contractor must make any changes to the draft remediation plan reasonably requested by the Principal and resubmit the draft remediation plan to the Principal for approval within 5 days of the request.
Without limiting its other obligations under the Contract, the Contractor must comply with the remediation plan as approved by the Principal, and provide reports and other information about the Contractor’s progress in implementing the remediation plan as reasonably requested by the Principal.
A failure by the Contractor to comply with its obligations under this clause will be a breach of the Contract. The Principal’s rights under this clause are in addition to and do not otherwise limit any other rights the Principal may have under the Contract. The performance by the Contractor of its obligations under this clause will be at no additional cost to the Principal.
For the purposes of this clause 66, 'Significant Event' means:
any adverse comments or findings made by a court, commission, tribunal or other statutory or professional body regarding the conduct or performance of the Contractor or its officers, employees, agents or subcontractors that impacts or could be reasonably perceived to impact on their professional capacity, capability, fitness or reputation; or
any other significant matters, including the commencement of legal, regulatory or disciplinary action involving the Contractor or its officers, employees, agents or subcontractors, that may adversely impact on compliance with DFAT or Commonwealth policy, legislation or DFAT or the Commonwealth’s reputation.
   Ethical Conduct
The Contractor acknowledges and agrees that the Principal observes high ethical standards in the conduct of its activities, and the Principal is relying on the Contractor observe high ethical standards in the performance of its obligations and duties in the execution of the Works (as reasonably determined by the Superintendent). 
The Contractor must ensure that each of its officers, employees and subcontractors observe similar standards of ethical conduct.
Without limiting the generality of the previous paragraph, the Contractor must not engage in (and will use its best endeavours to ensure that its employees and subcontractor’s does not engage in):
conduct that is misleading, fraudulent or deceptive;
conduct that is illegal under the laws of Australia or Papua New Guinea;
conduct that amounts to bribery; or
conduct that amounts to terrorism or that supports or assists terrorism.
   Workplace Gender Equality 
The Contractor must, where the Contractor is a 'relevant employer' for the purposes of the Workplace Gender Equality Act 2012 (Cth) ('WGE Act'):
comply with the Contractor’s obligations, if any, under the WGE Act; and
not enter into a subcontract with an entity named in a report tabled in the Australian Parliament by the Director of the Workplace Gender Equality Agency as a supplier that has not complied with the WGE Act.
If the Contractor is a 'relevant employer' under the WGE Act and become[s] non-compliant with the WGE Act, the Contractor must notify the Superintendent as soon as reasonably practical.
The Contractor must provide a current letter of compliance from the Workplace Gender Equality Agency within 18 Months from the Date of the Contract and following this, annually, to the Superintendent.
Compliance with the WGE Act does not relieve the Contractor from the Contractor’s responsibility to comply with the Contractor’s other obligations under this Contract.
   Applicable Laws and Polices
Without limiting any other provision of this Contract, the Contractor must (to the fullest extent possible, regardless of its legal application) comply with the following legislation of the Commonwealth of Australia ('Commonwealth') and policies in connection with this Contract and when performing the Works under the Contract, as if all the Works under the Contract were performed, and this Contract were being performed for the Commonwealth in the Australian Capital Territory:

Standards Australia
Standards Australia is an independent company, limited by guarantee, which prepares and publishes most of the voluntary technical and commercial standards used in Australia. These standards are developed through an open process of consultation and consensus, in which all interested parties are invited to participate. Through a Memorandum of Understanding with the Commonwealth government, Standards Australia is recognized as Australia’s peak national standards body.  For further information on Standards Australia visit us at
www.standards.org.au
Australian Standards
Australian Standards are prepared by committees of experts from industry, governments, consumers and other relevant sectors. The requirements or recommendations contained in published Standards are a consensus of the views of representative interests and also take account of comments received from other sources. They reflect the latest scientific and industry experience. Australian Standards are kept under continuous review after publication and are updated regularly to take account of changing technology.
International Involvement
Standards Australia is responsible for ensuring that the Australian viewpoint is considered in the formulation of international Standards and that the latest international experience is incorporated in national Standards. This role is vital in assisting local industry to compete in international markets. Standards Australia represents Australia at both ISO (The International Organization for Standardization) and the International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC).
Electronic Standards
All Australian Standards are available in electronic editions, either downloaded individually from our web site, or via On-Line and DVD subscription services. For more information phone 1300 65 46 46 or visit Standards Web Shop at
www.standards.com.au
GPO Box 5420 Sydney  NSW  2001
Administration  Phone (02) 8206 6000 Fax (02) 8206 6001 Email mail@standards.com.au
Customer Service  Phone 1300 65 46 46 Fax 1300 65 49 49 Email sales@standards.com.au

OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
PART 2. 
SCOPE OF REQUIREMENTS 
1. 
Scope of Requirements 
 
1.1. 
Project Overview 
1.1.1. 
The Australian High Commission (AHC) Staff Residential Compound (SRC) 
Konedobu, is located approximately 3 kilometres (km) north of Port Moresby’s CBD 
in an elevated area. Built in 1993, it comprises 45 residences in a mix of 
townhouses, duplexes, and apartments with three- and four-bedroom layouts. 
Townhouses are two storeys, and apartment buildings across many levels, 
connected by external stairs to suit the site’s topography. Construction uses 
concrete blockwork, concrete, and timber framing. The SRC spans 11,315 square 
metres (m²) of built area within 31,000m² of landscaped grounds and includes 
communal amenities, recreation areas, security features, open spaces, playgrounds, 
and essential services such as potable water tanks and backup generators.  
1.1.2. 
The compound was built as a family residence compound and provides 
accommodation for staff employed by Commonwealth agencies including DFAT in 
Port Moresby. 
1.1.3. 
Whilst the residences have been maintained, albeit consistent with the 1990’s date 
of construction, the object of the Project is to upgrade and modernise all the 
properties to bring them up to a fit-for-purpose condition. A staged refurbishment 
delivery program will be established to minimise disruption to occupants and assist 
with completing works in the minimum possible time. 
1.1.4. 
The intent of the refurbishment is to use durable, climate-appropriate materials that 
can be sourced and maintained locally or within the region. The design standardises 
layouts and finishes, improves maintainability, and applies a repeatable “kit-of-parts” 
approach to enable interchangeable components and efficient repairs.  
1.1.5. 
The works will be carried as a staged rolling program. A Pilot Project was undertaken 
as a due diligence exercise and provided lessons learnt that have been incorporated 
into the refurbishment of the remaining 39 residences (this Project). The remainder of 
the works will be staged over future years. 
1.2. 
Project Scope 
1.2.1. 
The general refurbishment scope for each residence includes: 
a. 
The current design, building layout and arrangement while generally fixed,        
the contractor should undertake an exercise of due diligence/verification of the 
design against each dwelling. Ensuring any differences or are identified 
including existing fixtures and services. This includes kitchen and bathroom 
layouts and product specifications that will improve overall durability and 
longevity of the design. Design is not bespoke but is a kit of parts (modules) that 
are repeatable in each residence. 
b. 
Demolition of internal surfaces and fittings as required. Over the past 30 years, 
various works have been undertaken to each residence which are inconsistent 
due what could be sourced within country and the available workforce. Each 
house has varying floor and wall finishes, light fittings, split systems and power 
outlets, etc. 
 
 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 3  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
c. 
While the properties will be handed over vacant, several elements will be 
required to be salvaged from the demolition. For example, ceiling fans, lights, 
connected appliances, AC units, pelmets, curtains, fire hydrants, safes. etc. 
Items to be salvaged and will be confirmed by DFAT for the dilapidation report.  
d. 
Demolition of internal surfaces, including flooring, fittings and redundant 
services. 
e. 
Removal and treatment of mouldy plasterboard walls and ceilings 
f. 
New wall linings, and insulation to ceilings and selected walls 
g. 
Supply and install new kitchens and appliances, relocate services to suit new 
layout and appliances. 
h. 
Supply and install new robes, walk in robes, while working around existing security 
hardware  
i. 
Refurbishment of wet areas (bathrooms, powder rooms, and laundries), 
including preparation of floors and walls, waterproofing, relocate services as 
required, supply and fit new tiles and fitments.  
j. 
Preparation of floor for new tiles throughout, noting that each residence has 
inconsistent floor levels (potentially to each room). Where inconsistent floor 
levels are present additional floor treatment/ leveling will be required to 
standardise design and layouts and eliminate trip hazards. 
k. 
Replace ceiling fans and replacement of existing air-conditioning with new 
energy-efficient refrigerated air conditioning systems and new bathroom and 
laundry and kitchen exhaust fans. 
l. 
Replace/add light fittings and switch plates, electrical outlets, all existing power 
and switch cover plates for a consistent installation. 
m. Replace/ upgrade distribution boards. This also includes electrical re-wiring if 
there are any non-compliance discovered with the existing installation. Inclusion 
of surge protection to be incorporate into switch boards. 
n. 
Replace solar hot water system  
o. 
Replace/ upgrade fire services configuration to meet code compliance. Including 
replacement of fire hydrants and blankets. 
p. 
Upgrade phone and data cable connection points, and install a conduit for future 
capability upgrade from roof to living room.  
q. 
New stair handrails and balustrades, and refurbish the existing stair treads, 
providing an additional fall protection to the safe haven gate. 
r. 
New paint throughout. 
s. 
Rectification of any damage to the external painted brick façade as a result of the 
works. 
t. 
New double glazing and security screens to window openings, curtains and 
window furniture.

1.4. 
Proposed Project Stages and Phases 
1.4.1. 
Due to the requirement to continue to house residents on the site, it is proposed that 
between 4-6 dwellings are refurbished at any one time to enable a rolling staged program 
of works across 7 remaining stages. This approach minimises disruption to residents and 
significantly reduces the need for additional leased housing in the constrained rental 
market in Port Moresby. The indicative staging plan below is subject to change to respond 
to operational requirements or successful tenderer identified efficiencies in planning and 
delivery. As the project progresses the staging plan may be reviewed and amended to 
meet operational needs.

1.6. 
Design and Construct (D&C) Contractors Roles and Responsibilities: 
1.6.1. 
Responsibilities: 
a. 
The Tenderer is to allow for the inclusion of all design elements, to ensure 
completeness and functionality of design. 
b. 
DFAT has provided designs to 90% and appropriate supporting documentation. 
The successful Tenderer will need to undertake a due diligence exercise to 
confirm the accuracy of the drawings, coordinating their own design consultants to 
bring the drawings up to 100% detailed design for DFAT’s approval before 
progressing through to construction. DFATS Architects and Engineers will Peer 
Review all design amendments. This also provides an opportunity for the 
Tenderer to propose alternative solutions (i.e. materials, details, etc.) that can 
increase efficiency and constructability. 
c. 
The Contractor will be responsible for all aspects of the project delivery for each 
residence, including: 
i. 
Submission of documentation as required for building permits, local 
authority approvals, completion certificates and certificates of occupancy            
(if required). 
ii. 
In country building contractors/ subcontractors. 
iii. Suitably qualified services trades - plumbing, electrician, and mechanical. 
iv. Other specialised trades as required for internal fit-out works. 
v. 
Others sub-contractors as considered necessary by the Tenderer to ensure 
the requirements of this Contract can be achieved. 
vi. Maintaining a Project Program of works.  
vii. A fulltime Construction Project Manager/ Site Supervisor with appropriate 
supporting staff to direct and instruct trades and oversee all works during the 
delivery phase as well as. 
viii. Reporting obligations under the contract and as reasonably requested by 
the PMCA. 
ix. If local trades are used, they need task direction and supervision. 
d. 
Shall form and manage a self-motivated experienced team that will drive the due 
diligence process and delivery of the Project. The Contractor is responsible for all 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 10  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
aspects of project management and delivery to provide a turnkey solution for each 
residence. 
e. 
Is responsible for due diligence, stakeholder engagement (including residences if 
required), logistic, local authority engagement and building approval(s), 
procurement of trades and materials, construction, testing and commissioning, 
certification and handover takeover activities. DFAT has engaged a design and 
engineering team to assist with the review and oversight of the Contractor’s 
activities. 
1.6.2. 
Deliverables: The Contractor: 
a. 
Is to undertake due diligence at all residences (internal and external) and produce 
a report detailing findings, (dilapidation report). Upon acceptance of the report by 
DFAT and reengagement has occurred; the Tenderer is to work with their design 
and engineering consultants to address any discrepancies. The Tenderer should 
include allowance in the Program for obtaining approvals (if required) and 
importing materials with lead time items. 
b. 
Is responsible to undertake design input to uplift the drawings to 100% and Issued 
For Construction (IFC) drawings as part of the due diligence process. 
c. 
Shall continually monitor and identify problems, assess risk, and assist to facilitate 
early and cost-effective resolution to achieve delivery of the Project within the 
performance criteria, program, cost, and quality as specified in this RFT document 
and supporting attachments. 
d. 
Maintain quality control for the implementation of the designs during construction, 
and to that end they will be required to fully participate in a system of regular and 
progressive certification of the quality of the work carried out. 
e. 
Is to deliver the works in compliance with Australian Building Codes and 
standards, as well as consider corresponding any particular local codes and 
standards. Where compliance with Australian requirements is not possible, the 
Contractor is to propose, for approval by DFAT and its consultants, an alternate 
solution that provides comparable performance without introducing risk. 
Assessment and approvals of all deviation is to be documented and detailed in the 
Compliance Gap Analysis by the Contractor. All deviations from Australian 
Standards are to be reviewed and approved by the Project Manager/ Contract 
Administrator (PMCA) prior to implementation. 
f. 
Determine statutory and non-statutory authorities that need to be consulted, and 
the requirements of these authorities which must be incorporated. The Contractor 
must prepare a list of authorities the Contractor will consult with and seek approval 
from (complete with contacts) and must program and monitor all consultation with 
authorities and all authority approval processes. Any requirements identified by 
authorities must be submitted by the Contractor for approval by the PMCA. 
g. 
Develop and manage a schedule/ program the program must separately identify 
each location of the Works, start-up, design development, mobilisation, 
procurements, construction, commissioning and Defects Liability Period (DLP).  
The program must include a project calendar identifying working days, rostered 
days off (RDO’s) and public holidays, the maximum major timescale unit must be 
weeks, task durations are to be in calendar days, each subcontractor must be 
identified, and all tasks are to be linked to show dependencies to other tasks.

OFFICIAL
Most important, it also must show a critical path of tasks/activities and a percent 
complete against each task. 
1.7. 
Material and Product Selection 
1.7.1. 
Considerations: 
a. 
The deterioration of building materials in tropical climates is more extreme and 
generally different to temperate climates. The main deteriorative influences in 
tropical climates are high temperatures, moisture, high humidity, solar radiation, 
insects and micro-organisms, and in marine environments, the effects of salt. 
Serviceability and maintenance capabilities in country must be taken into 
consideration in remote locations. Local specific technical training for the ongoing 
serviceability and maintenance must be implemented. 
b. 
When confirming specified or proposing alternative fixtures and fittings, the repairs, 
maintenance and availability of spares must also be examined. An example may 
be wall mounted hot water units being favoured over instantaneous hot/cold taps at 
sinks due to the lack of maintenance required and in country maintenance 
programs available. 
1.7.2. 
Corrosive Resistance: 
a. 
Some materials inherently possess corrosion resistance properties whilst other 
materials rely on methods and treatments to be applied to them for them to be 
considered corrosion resistant. 
b. 
The atmospheric corrosivity category for the proposed project is defined in AS 4312 
and the AS/NZS 2312 series as: 
c. 
Exterior atmospheric corrosivity category: C5-M Very High - Marine 
d. 
Interior atmospheric corrosivity category: C1 Dry indoors 
1.8. 
Prevention of Mould and Mildew: 
1.8.1. 
Appropriate material selection and installation to provide the most appropriate solution 
to combat mould and mildew as there is frequent precipitation and high air humidity 
typical of tropical climates producing large amounts of moisture (the main prerequisite 
for mould growth). The temperatures in humid climates are usually high throughout 
most of the year and contribute to the quick reproduction of mould spores. 
1.8.2. 
Appropriate air conditioning and ventilation solution. The excessive moisture combined 
with the right temperatures allows for extensive mould growth. Prolonged damp 
conditions can lead to the colonisation of building materials and HVAC systems by 
moulds bacteria, wood-decaying moulds and insect pests (e.g., termites and carpenter 
ants). Humid indoor air may condense on the cool backside of wall linings that covers 
an exterior wall, providing ideal conditions for mould to grow. 
1.8.3. 
Keep building materials dry during construction to prevent mould growth. 
1.8.4. 
Waterproofing: Apply waterproofing membranes in wet areas to prevent water ingress. 
Ensure compliance with AS 3740 - Waterproofing of domestic wet areas. 
1.8.5. 
Identification and Assessment: Prior to any new works being undertaken, Identify and 
assess the extent of mould contamination for existing conditions, including cleaning and 
removing mould using appropriate methods and personal protective equipment (PPE). 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 12  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
1.9. 
Engagement and Communication 
1.9.1. 
In order to facilitate timely and relevant progress, the Contractor must communicate 
effectively with stakeholders, noting the DFAT PMCA is to be informed of all 
communication. In the first instance, communication will be through the on site Project 
Manager to inform delivery schedules, weekly ‘look ahead reports’ as well as issues 
which may affect the occupants of the site. Where there are planned or scheduled 
significant meetings or liaison with stakeholders (Departments representatives), the 
Contractor must advise the PMCA in advance, within an appropriate timeframe, so that 
they can arrange to attend as required. All formal presentations, and the like, must be 
arranged through the PMCA, and chaired and minuted by the Contractor. 
1.9.2. 
Required meetings and workshops as a minimum: 
a. 
Monthly / Fortnightly / Weekly meetings to provide Project specific information to 
the DFAT, the PMCA and Contractor/s (if required) and to discuss the Project 
specific issues as described under the key activities. These issues will align with 
the PMCA and Contractor’s Monthly Report. 
b. 
The Contractor must prepare a weekly report and an overall monthly report with a 
detailed status of the project, not limited to: design, procurement, shipment update 
on long lead time items, construction onsite, risk, WHS reporting etc. 
c. 
The Contractor must prepare for, attend and participate in meetings as directed by 
the PMCA. 
d. 
Design meetings and workshops to focus on key design issues, program and 
progress, as required and gain acceptance from DFAT. 
e. 
Presentations to stakeholders as required. 
f. 
Commissioning and handover meetings as required. 
1.9.3. 
Without the prior approval of the Contract Administrator, the Contractor must not consult 
directly with any Commonwealth and Host Nation stakeholders.

1.10. 
Project Structure  
 
1.11. 
Due Diligence Services 
1.11.1. 
Due to the age and disparate condition of the buildings at Konedobu, and the limited 
access to undertake extensive investigations of individual residences while still 
occupied, the scope or works includes a component of due diligence to ensure that 
individual apartment differences are captured typically around existing services.  
1.11.2. 
DFAT will provide Design drawings to 90%. The contractor is responsible to undertake 
due diligence review to uplift the drawings to 100%/ IFC drawings and opportunities of 
innovation for the Contractor to incorporate. 
1.11.3. 
The Contractor is to engage with DFAT throughout a due diligence stage for each of the 
‘stages’ (for construction), the final Design should reflect and build on the 90% DFAT 
Design provided, and any outcomes of the due diligence phase of works highlighting 
and reflecting any differences. 
1.11.4. 
While no significant differences are expected, the disparate nature of the maintenance 
and replacement of building service upgrades have resulted in differences between 
dwellings. For example, existing HVAC locations vary external to the buildings, 
proposed locations may need to be reviewed on a per dwelling basis to confirm optimal 
location. Power outlets and phone outlet locations vary in number and location. 
1.11.5. 
The Designs is to comply with Australian codes and standards, relevant statutory, 
regulatory, and departmental requirements. Products specified must be able to be 
serviced and maintained locally. 
1.11.6. 
The Contractor is required to provide the following for DFAT’s approval: 
a. 
Due Diligence/ Dilapidation/ Latent Condition Report. 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 14  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
b. 
Work with the Australian High Commission security team and engage their 
incumbent security provider (if required) to undertake a test and commissioning 
report on the existing security system prior to works. 
c. 
Proposed alternatives specifications and products. 
d. 
Detailed Due diligence report and Documentation as required outlining any 
discrepancies and documentation of proposed solutions. 
1.11.7. 
Design Due Diligence must be produced to a high standard to the satisfaction of the 
PMCA, clearly defining all construction details. Design Documentation must be         
100% complete so that detailing is not left for Trade Subcontractors to resolve. 
1.11.8. 
The Design Documentation must comply with the following as a minimum: 
a. 
Comprehensive definition of scope in drawings, specifications and schedules not 
relying on any assumptions of what should be allowed for by “common industry 
practice” or “best practice”. 
b. 
Accurate, clear and concise text notations, details and sections on drawings to 
assist interpretation of drawings. 
c. 
Use of exploded views, erection sequence diagrams, isometric views, insets, 
assembly diagrams as necessary to convey complex details. 
d. 
All amendments must be clouded on drawings and highlighted in schedules and 
specifications and cross-referenced to the notes in the amendment’s column. 
1.11.9. 
Format: All Design Documentation, including design development and subcontract 
documentation, must be submitted in hard copy, in electronic formats (CAD) and in PDF 
format, or as otherwise required by the PMCA. 
1.11.10. 
Errors and Omissions: The Contractor is to ensure that through design finalisation and 
due diligence, design co-ordination meetings and quality assurance of the design 
documentation are managed to ensure there are no errors or omissions within the 
design documentation. 
1.11.11. 
Value Management (VM): Review design and costing if required to enable DFAT to 
assess how the design is tracking against the offered price. VM workshops will be 
facilitated by the PMCA and Cost Planner and will be conducted during the Due 
diligence phase. 
1.12. 
Construction Services 
1.12.1. 
The general refurbishment scope for each residence includes: 
a. 
The current design, building layout and arrangement while generally fixed, the 
contractor should undertake an exercise of due diligence of the design against 
each dwelling. This includes kitchen and bathroom layouts and product 
specifications that will improve overall durability and longevity of the design. Design 
is not bespoke, but is a kit of parts (modules) that are repeatable in each 
residence. 
b. 
Demolition of internal surfaces and fittings as required. Over the past 30 years, 
various works have been undertaken to each residence which are inconsistent due 
what could be sourced within country and the available workforce. Each house has 
varying floor and wall finishes, light fittings, split systems and power outlets, etc. 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 15  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
c. 
While the properties will be handed over vacant, several elements will be required 
to be salvaged from the demolition. For example, ceiling fans, lights, connected 
appliances, AC units, pelmets, curtains. etc. Items to be salvaged and will be 
confirmed by DFAT for the dilapidation report. 
d. 
Demolition of internal surfaces, including flooring, fittings and redundant services. 
e. 
Removal and treatment of mouldy plasterboard walls and ceilings 
f. 
New wall linings, and insulation to ceilings and selected walls 
g. 
Supply and install new kitchens and appliances, relocate services to suit new layout 
and appliances. 
h. 
Supply and install new robes, walk in robes, while working around existing security 
hardware  
i. 
Refurbishment of wet areas (bathrooms, powder rooms, and laundries), including 
preparation of floors and walls, waterproofing, relocate services as required, supply 
and fit new tiles and fitments.  
j. 
Preparation of floor for new tiles throughout, noting that each residence has 
inconsistent floor levels (potentially to each room). Where inconsistent floor levels 
are present additional floor treatment/ leveling will be required to standardise 
design and layouts and eliminate trip hazards. 
k. 
Replace ceiling fans and Replacement of existing air-conditioning with new energy-
efficient refrigerated air conditioning systems and new bathroom and laundry 
exhaust fans. 
l. 
Replace/add light fittings and switch plates, electrical outlets, all existing power and 
switch cover plates for a consistent installation. 
m. Replace/ upgrade distribution boards. This also includes electrical re-wiring if there 
are any non-compliance discovered with the existing installation. Inclusion of surge 
protection to be incorporate into switch boards. 
n. 
Replace solar hot water system  
o. 
Replace/ upgrade fire services configuration to meet code compliance 
p. 
Upgrade phone and data cable connection points and install a conduit for future 
capability upgrade from roof to living room.

New stair handrails and balustrades, and refurbish the existing stair treads, providing 
additional fall protection to the safe haven gate. 
r. 
New paint throughout. 
s. 
Rectification of any damage to the external painted brick façade as a result of the 
works. 
t. 
New double glazing and security screens to window openings, curtains and window 
furniture. 
u. 
Exiting security system is not to be altered or touched unless in coordination and 
consultation with the AHC security team. The security system is to be handed back 
over to the AHC team at the end of the project in the same operating condition as it 
was in the testing and commissioning report during Due Diligence phase.

1.12.2. 
Mandated Standards and Codes:  
Applicable Australian (AS) and New Zealand (NZ) Standards and Codes are to be used 
on this project due to their high level of stipulated requirements, and each element is to 
reference the appropriate standard accordingly. Ensure the finished product complies 
with the National Construction Code (NCC).  
1.12.3. 
Mechanical Systems 
a. 
Refer to Attachment C  
b. 
Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HVAC) to AS1668: Ensure the HVAC 
system is designed to provide adequate cooling for each unit. Use energy-efficient 
systems that are low maintenance and can be serviced and repaired in country.  
c. 
Ventilation: Ensure proper ventilation in all areas, including bathrooms, powder 
rooms, laundries, and kitchens, to maintain indoor air quality. Use exhaust fans and 
consider mechanical ventilation systems where natural ventilation is insufficient. 
For example, the Building Code of Australia (BCA) requires an airflow of at least 20 
litres per second for laundries, 25 litres per second for toilets and 50 litres per 
second for bathrooms. 
d. 
Energy Efficiency: Follow the guidelines in HB 276 for energy-efficient installation of 
heating, cooling, and air conditioning equipment. 
e. 
Insulation: Properly insulate ductwork and pipes to improve energy efficiency and 
reduce noise. 
f. 
Condensate Drainage: Ensure proper drainage of condensate to prevent water 
damage. The material used for the drain must be suitable for the purpose and 
resistant to sunlight if exposed. 
g. 
Condensation: Appropriate material selection and insulation is the most appropriate 
solution to combat condensation. In addition to insulating ceilings, walls, and other 
structural elements, it’s advisable to insulate the surfaces that are most prone to 
forming condensation as well, such as metal door frames. The use of “vapour 
permeable” barriers in the building envelope are required to control water 
permeability. Selection of the vapour barrier is critical to maintain drier conditions, 
prevent risks associated with condensation, and reduces moisture and mould 
related issues. 
h. 
Outdoor Unit Placement: Securely fix outdoor units and consider their placement to 
avoid noise issues and ensure efficient operation. 
i. 
Ensure any service penetration of the external walls connecting condensers is 
adequately sealed where transitioning through the external envelope. 
j. 
Noise Levels: Ensure the installation of both indoor and external units complies with 
local noise regulations to minimise disturbance to residents and neighbours. 
1.12.4. 
Electrical Systems 
a. 
Refer to Attachment C  
b. 
Wiring and Circuitry: Use high-quality wiring that complies with AS/NZS 3000. 
Ensure circuits are appropriately rated for the load they will carry. 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 17  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
c. 
Lighting: Design to AS/NZS 1158:1. Install energy-efficient lighting, such as LED 
fixtures, and ensure adequate lighting in all areas. Use moisture- resistant fixtures 
in wet areas like bathrooms etc. 
d. 
Safety Features: Include safety features like Residual Current Devices (RCDs) and 
surge protectors. Ensure all electrical installations comply with AS/NZ safety 
regulations. 
1.12.5. 
Plumbing Systems 
a. 
Refer to Attachment C  
b. 
Water Supply: Ensure a reliable and efficient water supply system, including hot 
and cold-water services. Use materials that comply with AS/NZS 3500. 
c. 
Sanitary Plumbing: Install sanitary plumbing systems that efficiently handle waste 
and prevent blockages. Ensure compliance with the Plumbing Code of Australia 
(PCA) 5. 
d. 
Drainage: install effective wastewater drainage systems to prevent flooding and 
water damage. Ensure proper grading, location and installation of drains. 
e. 
Water Efficiency: Use water-efficient fixtures and fittings to reduce water 
consumption and comply with sustainability standards. 
f. 
Replace solar hot water system 
1.12.6. 
For Architectural and Interior components, refer to Attachment C –Architectural 
drawings Finishes Schedule, Attachment C – Fittings, Fixtures and Equipment 
Schedule, and Attachment C – General Specification  
1.12.7. 
The Contractor is to allow for provision of a separate office for the Superintendent’s 
onsite Project/Site Manager office to include for a Desk, Chair, meeting room, AC 
power, data, storage cabinets etc for the duration of the works. 
1.12.8. 
All materials are to be sourced and supplied by the contractor. DFAT will require the 
contractor to provide a Detailed Procurement Plan as part of their tender response. 
Consider the ESD properties of materials and equipment sourced and utilised in the 
execution of the fit-out. Solutions should consider the total lifecycle approach, including 
sustainable design and energy efficiency, reducing the environmental impact during 
construction, and minimising and simplifying enduring maintenance of the output 
facilities. The ESD process is to pay particular attention to the location, the climatic 
conditions, and the site and its associated constraints.

1.12.10. 
Work Health and Safety (WHS):  
a. 
WHS of all personnel is of primary concern at all times. The design and 
construction of the works must provide the end user with a working environment in 
conformity with all relevant statutory requirements and guidelines. 
b. 
The DFAT WHS Policy requires the Contractor to undertake the following: 
i. 
Australian-based contracted organisations have legislated duties stipulated in 
the relevant Australian State or Territory WHS legislation. However, overseas 
contracted organisations may have their own form of WHS legislation.  
ii. 
The Contractor must: 
• 
Comply with relevant WHS legislation and contract requirements. 
• 
Develop and implement their WHS systems to protect their workers and 
other persons from injury and/or illness as a result of the work. 
• 
Appropriately manage health and safety risks by cooperating and 
coordinating activities to eliminate or minimise those risks. 
• 
Provide their workers with information, instruction, training and 
supervision relevant to the work. 
• 
Inform the department when a worker loses a competency, certificate or 
licence. 
• 
Notify the Commonwealth Representative, onsite Project/Site Manager 
and the Contract Administrator immediately of all WHS incidents and 
undertake an investigation. 
• 
Notify the relevant local health and safety regulator of notifiable incidents. 
• 
Effectively communicate and consult with their workers and the 
department throughout the life of the contract. 
• 
Provide the department with a WHS assurance report in accordance with 
the contract. 
• 
carry out the Contractor's Activities safely and in a manner that does not 
put the health and safety of persons at risk; 
• 
carry out the Contractor's Activities in a manner that protects property; and 
• 
comply with any direction issued by the Contract Administrator to change 
its manner of working or to cease working if the Contract Administrator 
reasonably considers there is a risk to the health and safety of people or 
damage to property arising from the Contractor's Activities;  
iii. 
The contracted organisation will implement these requirements through the 
implementation of the contract and documented in the WHS Plan. 
iv. Should a notifiable incident occur onsite the contractor must; 
• 
immediately provide the Contract Administrator with a copy of the 
notice required to be provided to the relevant Commonwealth, or Host 
Nation regulator; 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 20  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
• 
promptly provide the Contract Administrator with a copy of all witness 
statements and the investigation report relating to the notifiable 
incident; 
• 
promptly provide the Contract Administrator with copies of any notice(s) 
or other documentation issued by the relevant Commonwealth, 
Australian State or Territory regulator or Host Nation regulator; and 
• 
within 10 days of the date of notification to the relevant Commonwealth, 
Australian State or Territory regulator or Host Nation regulator, provide 
the Contract Administrator with a summary of the related investigations, 
actions to be taken and any impact on the Contract that may result from 
the notifiable incident;  
1.12.11. 
Safe Work Method Statement (SWMS): 
a. 
In addition to any requirement under the Contractor’s WHS Plan, if DFAT considers 
an activity to be High Risk, the Contractor undertaking the activity must provide, in 
writing, to the DFAT a detailed description of the Work, a detailed assessment of 
the risks, and the specific arrangements and methods which the Contractor 
proposes to adopt for the execution of the Work (Safe Work Method Statement or 
similar). Such risks may involve, but not be limited to, risks to the environment, 
health, safety, damage to property or any existing installations or services.                   
The Contractor must then comply with the approved SWMS. 
b. 
Without limiting the Conditions of Contract or the Special Conditions, all Asbestos 
that is to be removed from Site must be in accordance with: 
i. 
All applicable Statutory Requirements, including WHS Legislation. 
ii. 
All relevant DFAT policy. 
iii. The Code of Practice for the Management and Control of Asbestos; 
iv. The Worksafe Australia Asbestos Code of Practice: The Safe Removal of 
Asbestos. 
v. 
The Guide to the Control of Asbestos Hazards in Buildings and Structures; and 
vi. The Guidance Note on the Membrane Filter Method for Estimating Airborne 
Asbestos Dust. 
1.12.12. 
Request for Information (RFI) 
a. 
Without limiting the Conditions of Contract, when submitting any RFI the Contractor 
must clearly state that the Contractor has reviewed all available documentation to 
address the issue directly before submitting the RFI and must include: 
i. 
A detailed description of the issue for which the RFI is being made. 
ii. 
References to all applicable documentation. 
iii. All sketches and/or photographs outlining the issue (if applicable). 
iv. The initial request for information from the subcontractor (if applicable). 
v. 
All relevant drawings, specifications references pertaining to the issue (if 
applicable). 
vi. Any other applicable information available to the Contractor.

OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
vii. All RFIs must also include, wherever possible design proposals or options 
developed by the Contractor for resolving the issue. The Contractor must 
endorse all RFIs before submission. 
1.12.13. 
Approvals 
a. 
The Contractor is deemed to have allowed in the Contract price and in its Program 
for the submission to the Project/Site Manager of all necessary and required 
samples, prototypes and workshop drawings, and for reasonable time necessary 
for the Project/Site Manager to assess and respond, having regard to the extent, 
complexity and nature of the items submitted for approval. The Contractor must 
submit further samples, and additional or amended information as may be directed 
by the Project/Site Manager. 
b. 
The Contractor shall also be deemed to have allowed for the provision of any 
further information that the Project/Site Manager may reasonably request in order 
to assess submissions, and for resubmission where applicable of those items not 
approved, including any amendments to workshop drawings. The Contractor will be 
deemed to have allowed for all these circumstances in its Contract Price and in its 
program. 
c. 
The Contractor is not permitted to commence work affected by samples, prototypes 
or the works identified by workshop drawings until they have been approved by the 
Project/Site Manager. Approval by the Project/Site Manager does not affect the 
responsibilities of the Contractor under the Contract. 
d. 
Approval by the Project/Site Manager does not affect the responsibilities of the 
Contractor under the Contract. 
1.12.14. 
Samples, Prototypes and Shop Drawings:  
The Contractor is to provide samples as requested by the PMCA and design consultants 
with all shop drawings for review approval by PMCA and Design Consultants. No works 
are to begin until all shop drawings have been received and reviewed by the PMCA. 
Upon receiving written confirmation and comments from the PMCA of the received 
shop drawings works can then begin. 
1.12.15. 
Consistency:  
For the whole quantity of each material or product the Contactor must use the same 
manufacturer or source and provide consistent type, size, quality, and appearance. 
1.12.16. 
Product Guarantees: 
The Contactor shall obtain for the benefit of Commonwealth Government of Australia, 
product guarantees, warranties and the like where these are available from the 
manufacturers, suppliers and/or sub-contractors. Such guarantees and warranties 
should be included in the operational and maintenance manual for said product. 
1.13. 
Works-As-Executed or As-Built Drawings: 
1.13.1. 
The Contactor shall provide Works As-Executed (As-Builts) and as installed drawings in 
English at the completion of each stage at handover, which will need to be an accurate 
record of the work performed by the Contractor or its sub-contractors for the entirety of 
the Works including shop drawings. These drawings are to be true reflection of the as 
built delivered, and not s rebadging of the design drawing exercise.

1.13.2. 
Further to the particular requirements of the Technical Specifications and the

1.14. 
Handover - Operation and Maintenance Manuals (O&M Manuals):  
1.14.1. 
The Contractor must fully co-operate with the Contract Administrator, the 
Commonwealth and relevant Host Nation agencies or authorities, and otherwise to take 
all steps necessary to ensure the timely, efficient and comprehensive and smooth: 
a. 
Commissioning of the Works or each Stage. 
b. 
Handover of the Works or each Stage to its intended occupants. 
c. 
Occupation, use, operation and maintenance of the Works or each Stage by its 
intended occupants.   
1.14.2. 
Without limiting paragraph (5.14.1), the Contractor shall also: 
a. 
Comply with its Commissioning Training and Handover Plan. 
b. 
Witnessed Commissioning certificates for services including but not limited to fire 
systems, plumbing, electrical. 
c. 
Arrange and coordinate with post for recommissioning and testing of the security 
system to ensure no damage has occurred during works and it is still functioning as 
per its condition at initial site handover. 
d. 
As and when required by the Contract Administrator, provide the Commonwealth 
and such persons from the Host Nation or otherwise as notified by the Contract 
Administrator, with such other specific assistance as may be required by the 
Contract Administrator to facilitate the commissioning, training handover and other 
activities set out in paragraph (a), including providing the occupants and Other 
Contractors with any training required to operate and maintain the Works or the 
Stage. 
e. 
As a condition precedent to Completion, hand to the Contract Administrator or 
other persons nominated by the Contract Administrator all Project Documents 
which are required for the use, operation and maintenance of the Works or the 
relevant Stage.

c.

d. 
Text: Include all necessary manufacturers original quality printed data, associated 
diagrams.

with equipment; 
v.

procedures;

alignment and adjustment procedures. Provide logical sequence of instructions 
for each procedure; and 
vii. schedule of spares recommended to be held on site along with one set of 
spares, being those items subject to wear or deterioration and which may 
involve the Employer in extended deliveries when replacements are required. 
Include complete nomenclature and model numbers, and local sources of 
supply. 
i. 
Certificates: Copies of suppliers warranties (or guarantees), Copies of 
manufacturers warranties, Copies of Certificates from authorities, Product

a. 
As required by the Works Description.

throughout and at the end of the Defects Liability Period in a condition fit for their 
intended purpose.

Without limiting paragraphs (a) and (b), in accordance with the Commissioning 
Training and Handover Plan.

accreditation appropriate to the provision of the services for which they are responsible 
for the duration of the Contract.

During the term of the agreement, the Contractor may have access to private and

personnel. The Contractor must ensure that any confidential or personal information is 
treated as private and confidential at all times. Such information is not to be divulged to

1.18. 
Required Standards of Performance 
1.18.1. 
Industry and Legislative Requirements 
a. 
The Services must be carried out in accordance with the industry standards as 
stipulated by Local Authority. 
b. 
Performance in accordance with these industry standards must be to the 
satisfaction of the DFAT or relevant Commonwealth Entity and Commonwealth, 
State, local government and international legislative requirements. The Contractor 
is expected to conduct all of the Services in a manner that enhances the overall 
quality and performance of the activities with which the Services are associated. 
1.19. 
Regulatory Requirements 
1.19.1. 
The provision of the Services may require the Contractor to observe various Australian 
and international regulatory or legislative requirements. These may include privacy, 
security, hygiene, environmental, energy and resource usage, industrial, quarantine, 
heritage and work, health safety requirements and extend to such considerations as 
processing, handling, storing, using, and transporting various materials and 
substances. The Contractor must strictly observe such requirements and meet any 
associated business registration and licensing at their own cost. The PMCA or Project 
superintendent may require the Contractor to submit evidence confirming the 
satisfaction of these requirements both prior to the commencement of the Contract and 
during the course of the agreement. 
1.20. 
Conditions Applying to the Delivery of the Services 
1.20.1. 
Co-Ordination 
a. 
The Contractor is required to participate in formal coordination meetings as they 
apply to the delivery of the required services and the meeting of the associated 
contractual obligations. The Contractor’s attendance at the meetings will be at no 
additional cost to DFAT. 
1.20.2. 
Uplift and Delivery 
a. 
The Services are to be undertaken by the Contractor with the appropriate number 
of staff to be determined by the Contractor and noting requirements in clause 
5.14.2, in a professional manner, to ensure completion within the agreed 
timeframes. Appropriate timeframes will be established based on the volume of 
Household Goods to be removed/stored. 
1.20.3. 
Contractor Personnel 
a. 
The Contractor will be responsible for providing all personnel necessary for the 
proper conduct of the Services. All personnel utilised by the Contractor to perform 
the services are to be registered and licensed as required for the conduct of the 
particular service. The Contractor is to provide sufficient personnel to complete the 
Services within the timeframes agreed with the Commonwealth. 
1.20.4. 
Problem Resolution Procedures 
a. 
The Contractor must establish problem resolution procedures that, among other 
matters, provide for unresolved problems to be notified and escalated to the 
Contractor’s Escalation Representative as soon as possible, or 

 
PMSB 25007-C71 HWC RFT                                                                                                                                                  Page | 27  
OFFICIAL
OFFICIAL
b. 
Otherwise within 3 business days or other period of time notified by DFAT. As a 
minimum, these procedures are to provide a statement that specifies: 
i. 
the remedial action that would be proposed when a problem occurs; 
ii. 
the elapsed time for rectification; 
iii. how the problem would be escalated to rectification point; 
iv. what the escalation rectification points are from user to management; and 
v. 
the elapsed time for moving to the next higher rectification point. 
c. 
The problem resolution procedures established by the Contractor are separate to 
the agreed dispute resolution procedures set out in the draft contract. 
1.21. 
Security Requirements 
1.21.1. 
The Contractor must comply with: 
a. 
The Commonwealth Protective Security Policy. 
b. 
Any directions issued by DFAT in relation to security.  
1.21.2. 
Overseas Security 
a. 
The Services are required in some countries that may pose a higher security risk to 
both Contractor Personnel and DFAT staff. The Contractor must agree to take 
steps to ensure that these risks are minimised and that local subcontractors and 
their staff are engaged using sound commercial practices.
